People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is piled up at once—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, stuff around the house I keep starting and not finishing… and then I start thinking I’m just failing as a husband and as a dad.

Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.

I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.

I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.

It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.

So, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”

I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.

It’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.

I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.

Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.

I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.

I’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.

I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.

Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Or at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting around because sitting still feels weirdly impossible. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that. It’s more like when a computer has too many processes running and everything freezes—sorry, I know I keep making it about computers. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I put things off because I’m worried I’ll do them wrong, but then that gets tangled up with regular procrastination, you know? Like with work, if there’s a hard email or a decision, I might circle around it for way too long, but I still usually end up doing it. And then I’ll get distracted by something else, like checking some random household thing that probably didn’t need checking right then.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding situations. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Or at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting around because sitting still feels weirdly impossible. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Like, maybe I’m behind, then maybe my manager notices, then maybe that turns into a bad review, and then somehow I’m twenty steps ahead thinking I’m going to lose my job and my family’s going to be worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. I’ll be trying to exercise and breathe and do the normal healthy-person things, and my brain is still running some awful background process. Sorry, that’s a computer metaphor again. But yeah, it repeats, and I don’t feel like I can just shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.\n\nI came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to sort of organize it, like make a list or prioritize, because that’s what I do at work when things get messy, but then the list itself becomes another thing I’m behind on. Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s a little like when something crashes at work and everyone says the whole system is down, but then you’re trying to figure out if it’s actually down or just one service acting up—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison. I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular situation or thing that triggers your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really one thing, no. I kind of wish it were, because then maybe I could just avoid it or deal with that one thing.\n\nIt’s more like… everything piles together. I came in because I can’t stop worrying about all of it at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then my brain turns that into, “Okay, so maybe I’m just failing as a husband and father.”\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly one trigger. It’s more like any one of those can set it off, and then it spreads.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I mean, yes—that’s basically why I’m here.

It’s like even when nothing has actually happened, my brain is still running checks on everything. My marriage, work, bills, the house stuff I keep starting and then dropping… and then it turns into this whole bigger conclusion that I’m failing as a husband and father.

And I know some of those are normal adult things to think about, but it feels like I can’t shut it off. Like I’m always looking for the thing I missed.

I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. Like if something breaks, you trace it, patch it, reboot it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is my head just won’t let go of any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “sudden,” exactly. I mean, I get really worked up, but it’s usually like… my brain starts spinning and then I’m deep into it before I even realize. \n\nWhen you say “peaked within minutes,” do you mean like out of nowhere, like flipping a switch? Because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that. It’s more like when a computer has too many processes running and everything freezes—sorry, I know I keep making it about computers. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I put things off because I’m worried I’ll do them wrong, but then that gets tangled up with regular procrastination, you know? Like with work, if there’s a hard email or a decision, I might circle around it for way too long, but I still usually end up doing it. And then I’ll get distracted by something else, like checking some random household thing that probably didn’t need checking right then.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding situations. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is piled up at once—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, stuff around the house I keep starting and not finishing… and then I start thinking I’m just failing as a husband and as a dad. \n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to problems having a clear fix, you know? Like if something breaks, you trace it, patch it, reboot it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is my head just won’t let go of any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “sudden,” exactly. I mean, I get really worked up, but it’s usually like… my brain starts spinning and then I’m deep into it before I even realize. \n\nWhen you say “peaked within minutes,” do you mean like out of nowhere, like flipping a switch? Because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that. It’s more like when a computer has too many processes running and everything freezes—sorry, I know I keep making it about computers. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Or at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting around because sitting still feels weirdly impossible. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Like, maybe I’m behind, then maybe my manager notices, then maybe that turns into a bad review, and then somehow I’m twenty steps ahead thinking I’m going to lose my job and my family’s going to be worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. I’ll be trying to exercise and breathe and do the normal healthy-person things, and my brain is still running some awful background process. Sorry, that’s a computer metaphor again. But yeah, it repeats, and I don’t feel like I can just shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.\n\nI came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to sort of organize it, like make a list or prioritize, because that’s what I do at work when things get messy, but then the list itself becomes another thing I’m behind on. Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s a little like when something crashes at work and everyone says the whole system is down, but then you’re trying to figure out if it’s actually down or just one service acting up—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison. I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular situation or thing that triggers your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really one thing, no. I kind of wish it were, because then maybe I could just avoid it or deal with that one thing.\n\nIt’s more like… everything piles together. I came in because I can’t stop worrying about all of it at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then my brain turns that into, “Okay, so maybe I’m just failing as a husband and father.”\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly one trigger. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I put things off because I’m worried I’ll do them wrong, but then that gets tangled up with regular procrastination, you know? Like with work, if there’s a hard email or a decision, I might circle around it for way too long, but I still usually end up doing it. And then I’ll get distracted by something else, like checking some random household thing that probably didn’t need checking right then.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding situations. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Or at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting around because sitting still feels weirdly impossible. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Like, maybe I’m behind, then maybe my manager notices, then maybe that turns into a bad review, and then somehow I’m twenty steps ahead thinking I’m going to lose my job and my family’s going to be worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. I’ll be trying to exercise and breathe and do the normal healthy-person things, and my brain is still running some awful background process. Sorry, that’s a computer metaphor again. But yeah, it repeats, and I don’t feel like I can just shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.\n\nI came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to sort of organize it, like make a list or prioritize, because that’s what I do at work when things get messy, but then the list itself becomes another thing I’m behind on. Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s a little like when something crashes at work and everyone says the whole system is down, but then you’re trying to figure out if it’s actually down or just one service acting up—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison. I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular situation or thing that triggers your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really one thing, no. I kind of wish it were, because then maybe I could just avoid it or deal with that one thing.\n\nIt’s more like… everything piles together. I came in because I can’t stop worrying about all of it at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then my brain turns that into, “Okay, so maybe I’m just failing as a husband and father.”\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly one trigger. It’s more like any one of those can set it off, and then it spreads.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, yes—that’s basically why I’m here.\n\nIt’s like even when nothing has actually happened, my brain is still running checks on everything. My marriage, work, bills, the house stuff I keep starting and then dropping… and then it turns into this whole bigger conclusion that I’m failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd I know some of those are normal adult things to think about, but it feels like I can’t shut it off. Like I’m always looking for the thing I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these everyday things, like work or finances, for more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more days than not, definitely. I came in because it feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once.\n\nIt’ll be work, like whether I’m slipping or missing something, and then bills, and then something around the house I started and didn’t finish. And then somehow it turns into my marriage and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father. It’s not even always tied to one actual problem—it’s like my mind just keeps pulling things into the pile.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about these everyday things for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt kind of started when the cloud migration at work really ramped up—sorry, that’s probably too much work detail—but around then everything just got more intense. And at home, the arguments started becoming more regular around the same time, so it’s hard to separate what started what. But yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive and persistent worry about everyday things for most days over the past 9 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days, yeah. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything all at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and dropping…\n\nAnd then I’ll be thinking about something simple, like a bill or a half-finished repair, and somehow I’m suddenly at “I’m failing as a husband and father.” It just keeps piling on, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is piled up at once—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, stuff around the house I keep starting and not finishing… and then I start thinking I’m just failing as a husband and as a dad. \n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to problems having a clear fix, you know? Like if something breaks, you trace it, patch it, reboot it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is my head just won’t let go of any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “sudden,” exactly. I mean, I get really worked up, but it’s usually like… my brain starts spinning and then I’m deep into it before I even realize. \n\nWhen you say “peaked within minutes,” do you mean like out of nowhere, like flipping a switch? Because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that. It’s more like when a computer has too many processes running and everything freezes—sorry, I know I keep making it about computers. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I put things off because I’m worried I’ll do them wrong, but then that gets tangled up with regular procrastination, you know? Like with work, if there’s a hard email or a decision, I might circle around it for way too long, but I still usually end up doing it. And then I’ll get distracted by something else, like checking some random household thing that probably didn’t need checking right then.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding situations. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Or at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting around because sitting still feels weirdly impossible. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Like, maybe I’m behind, then maybe my manager notices, then maybe that turns into a bad review, and then somehow I’m twenty steps ahead thinking I’m going to lose my job and my family’s going to be worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. I’ll be trying to exercise and breathe and do the normal healthy-person things, and my brain is still running some awful background process. Sorry, that’s a computer metaphor again. But yeah, it repeats, and I don’t feel like I can just shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.\n\nI came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to sort of organize it, like make a list or prioritize, because that’s what I do at work when things get messy, but then the list itself becomes another thing I’m behind on. Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s a little like when something crashes at work and everyone says the whole system is down, but then you’re trying to figure out if it’s actually down or just one service acting up—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison. I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular situation or thing that triggers your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really one thing, no. I kind of wish it were, because then maybe I could just avoid it or deal with that one thing.\n\nIt’s more like… everything piles together. I came in because I can’t stop worrying about all of it at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then my brain turns that into, “Okay, so maybe I’m just failing as a husband and father.”\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly one trigger. It’s more like any one of those can set it off, and then it spreads.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, yes—that’s basically why I’m here.\n\nIt’s like even when nothing has actually happened, my brain is still running checks on everything. My marriage, work, bills, the house stuff I keep starting and then dropping… and then it turns into this whole bigger conclusion that I’m failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd I know some of those are normal adult things to think about, but it feels like I can’t shut it off. Like I’m always looking for the thing I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these everyday things, like work or finances, for more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more days than not, definitely. I came in because it feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once.\n\nIt’ll be work, like whether I’m slipping or missing something, and then bills, and then something around the house I started and didn’t finish. And then somehow it turns into my marriage and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father. It’s not even always tied to one actual problem—it’s like my mind just keeps pulling things into the pile.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about these everyday things for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt kind of started when the cloud migration at work really ramped up—sorry, that’s probably too much work detail—but around then everything just got more intense. And at home, the arguments started becoming more regular around the same time, so it’s hard to separate what started what. But yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive and persistent worry about everyday things for most days over the past 9 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days, yeah. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything all at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and dropping…\n\nAnd then I’ll be thinking about something simple, like a bill or a half-finished repair, and somehow I’m suddenly at “I’m failing as a husband and father.” It just keeps piling on, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries spread across many areas of your life, such as work, finances, relationships, and daily tasks, without one specific area being the main focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s not one thing I can point to and say, “this is the problem.” It spreads everywhere.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep dropping… and then it all somehow turns into this bigger thing where I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, I’ll start with something small, like realizing I forgot to follow up on a repair, and then my brain jumps to money, then work, then whether I’m letting everyone down. It’s hard to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stop worrying once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very hard. I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, just solve this one issue, and then one concern grabs onto another.\n\nOr I’ll try to work out and breathe through it, you know, like people say to do, and suddenly I’m not even thinking about the exercise anymore. I’m twenty steps ahead, imagining a bad review at work turning into losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. It just keeps going, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is piled up at once—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, stuff around the house I keep starting and not finishing… and then I start thinking I’m just failing as a husband and as a dad. \n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to problems having a clear fix, you know? Like if something breaks, you trace it, patch it, reboot it—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is my head just won’t let go of any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “sudden,” exactly. I mean, I get really worked up, but it’s usually like… my brain starts spinning and then I’m deep into it before I even realize. \n\nWhen you say “peaked within minutes,” do you mean like out of nowhere, like flipping a switch? Because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that. It’s more like when a computer has too many processes running and everything freezes—sorry, I know I keep making it about computers. But yeah, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thing, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… I don’t know. Maybe? I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, refusing to leave the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I put things off because I’m worried I’ll do them wrong, but then that gets tangled up with regular procrastination, you know? Like with work, if there’s a hard email or a decision, I might circle around it for way too long, but I still usually end up doing it. And then I’ll get distracted by something else, like checking some random household thing that probably didn’t need checking right then.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding situations. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues related to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much headaches or stomach stuff that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. I’m just wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are locked up and I’m bracing for something.\n\nI’ll be between meetings and realize I’ve been pacing around the room, or at chess club I’m tapping the pieces over and over because sitting still feels impossible. Which is annoying, because chess is supposed to be this quiet, focused thing, and I’m there fidgeting like I’m waiting for a server to crash. So… yeah, there’s definitely tension, even if I don’t know about the headaches or stomach part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if “ignore or suppress” is exactly what I’m doing, but I’ll try to push it aside, like, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix the bug, don’t spiral.\n\nAnd then one thought kind of grabs onto the next. Like I’ll worry a review at work is going to go badly, and then somehow I’m already twenty steps ahead thinking that means I’ll lose my job, and then my family will be worse off because of me. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m just following the logic.\n\nEven when I’m working out, I’ll try to breathe and just count reps or whatever, but the same stuff keeps coming back in. It’s hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “restless” before, but… yeah. I feel wound tight most of the day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m sort of bracing for something.\n\nAnd I catch myself pacing between meetings, even if there’s no reason to be moving around. Which is embarrassing, because everyone else is just calmly thinking and I’m over there acting like I’m waiting for some emergency. So… yeah, restless is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or anxiety that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, “out of nowhere” is hard for me to pin down, because usually I can find some thread that led there if I sit and think about it long enough.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve had, like, a sudden attack of fear with no reason at all. It’s more that my mind starts connecting things, and then suddenly it feels huge. But I guess that’s not exactly what you’re asking.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it coming out of nowhere, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had what you would describe as a full-blown anxiety attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “full-blown,” honestly. I’ve definitely gotten really anxious, like worked up to the point where it feels hard to think straight, but when I hear “anxiety attack” I picture something very dramatic, like you see in movies, and I don’t know if that’s what’s happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to get through something and I can feel it building, but then I start thinking about whether I’m overreacting, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the original thing. It’s kind of like when a program throws one error and then suddenly you’re chasing ten other errors that are probably related but maybe not… sorry, that’s probably not helpful.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I’ve had a full-blown anxiety attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ll try to tell myself, okay, just look at the code in front of you, fix the thing that’s actually broken, don’t invent a whole disaster. But then one concern hooks onto another. Like, maybe I’m behind, then maybe my manager notices, then maybe that turns into a bad review, and then somehow I’m twenty steps ahead thinking I’m going to lose my job and my family’s going to be worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. I’ll be trying to exercise and breathe and do the normal healthy-person things, and my brain is still running some awful background process. Sorry, that’s a computer metaphor again. But yeah, it repeats, and I don’t feel like I can just shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really one specific thing, which is part of the problem.\n\nI came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, all the house projects I start and then don’t finish—or I guess “drop” is more accurate—and then it all turns into this bigger thing where I’m wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to sort of organize it, like make a list or prioritize, because that’s what I do at work when things get messy, but then the list itself becomes another thing I’m behind on. Sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But yeah, it’s mostly those areas: family, work, money, the house, and whether I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure where the line is between a panic attack and just getting really, really anxious.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and like I need to get out of whatever I’m doing, but when people say “panic attack,” I think of something very specific and intense, and I don’t know if I can honestly say that’s happened. I don’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nIt’s a little like when something crashes at work and everyone says the whole system is down, but then you’re trying to figure out if it’s actually down or just one service acting up—sorry, that’s probably not the best comparison. I guess my answer is: I don’t know that I’ve had a panic attack, not in a way I’d confidently call that.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular situation or thing that triggers your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really one thing, no. I kind of wish it were, because then maybe I could just avoid it or deal with that one thing.\n\nIt’s more like… everything piles together. I came in because I can’t stop worrying about all of it at once—my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then my brain turns that into, “Okay, so maybe I’m just failing as a husband and father.”\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly one trigger. It’s more like any one of those can set it off, and then it spreads.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, yes—that’s basically why I’m here.\n\nIt’s like even when nothing has actually happened, my brain is still running checks on everything. My marriage, work, bills, the house stuff I keep starting and then dropping… and then it turns into this whole bigger conclusion that I’m failing as a husband and father.\n\nAnd I know some of those are normal adult things to think about, but it feels like I can’t shut it off. Like I’m always looking for the thing I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these everyday things, like work or finances, for more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more days than not, definitely. I came in because it feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once.\n\nIt’ll be work, like whether I’m slipping or missing something, and then bills, and then something around the house I started and didn’t finish. And then somehow it turns into my marriage and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.
